- [ ] **Step 48: Convert `GfxViewer` to two states and one view**

In `crates/frontend/src/gfx.rs`, replace the imports, the struct and `new`:

```rust
use crate::gfxpanels::{self, View, ViewState};
use crate::keys::Actions;
use crate::sf1panels::Sf1ViewState;
use machine::video::compose::LayerMask;
use machine::video::layers::{Layer, MAP_TILES};
use machine::video::palette::PENS;
use machine::video::sf1::tilemap::MapKind;
use machine::video::sf1::{LayerMask as Sf1LayerMask, Plane};
use machine::video::tiles::TileKind;
use machine::Machine;

/// How far `[` and `]` move the palette cursor: one row of swatches.
///
/// A row rather than one entry, because the palette is 3072 entries on CPS-1 and
/// 1024 on SF1, and stepping through either one at a time is not navigation. Both
/// boards' colour schemes are 16 pens wide, so a 64-wide row is four of them.
const PAL_PAGE: usize = 64;

/// SF1's palette, for the same wrap CPS-1 does at [`PENS`].
///
/// A third of CPS-1's, so a shared constant would let `]` walk two thirds of the way
/// off the end of SF1's palette and show sixteen rows of entry 0.
const SF1_PENS: usize = machine::video::sf1::palette::ENTRIES;

/// The graphics viewer's whole state.
///
/// `on` is separate from the view: hiding and showing must not lose where you were
/// looking, because `F9` is how you compare the viewer's answer against the game's
/// own screen and you will press it repeatedly.
///
/// # Why `view` is hoisted out of the two states
///
/// The two boards' cursors are genuinely separate — a CPS-1 `TileKind` means nothing
/// to SF1 and an SF1 `Plane` means nothing to CPS-1 — but *which view* is chrome.
/// With a `view` inside each state, `F10` on one board would leave the other looking
/// somewhere else, and a save state loaded across a board change would open a
/// different panel than the one you were reading. One field cannot desynchronise, and
/// [`GfxViewer::view`] keeps its no-argument signature because of it.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct GfxViewer {
    /// Whether the box is drawn.
    on: bool,
    /// Which view is shown, on either board.
    view: View,
    /// Where CPS-1's four views are looking.
    cps1: ViewState,
    /// Where SF1's four views are looking.
    sf1: Sf1ViewState,
}
```

And in `new()`:

```rust
    pub fn new() -> Self {
        Self {
            on: false,
            view: View::Tiles,
            cps1: ViewState {
                view: View::Tiles,
                kind: TileKind::Tile16x16,
                layer: Layer::Scroll2,
                tile_at: 0,
                pal_at: 0,
                map_at: None,
                row: 0,
                mask: LayerMask::all(),
            },
            sf1: Sf1ViewState {
                view: View::Tiles,
                plane: Plane::Bg,
                map: MapKind::Bg,
                tile_at: 0,
                pal_at: 0,
                map_at: None,
                row: 0,
                mask: Sf1LayerMask::all(),
            },
        }
    }
```

⚠️ Each state still carries a `view` field, initialised to the same value the hoisted
one starts at. It is a *write-only* field here: `state()` and `sf1_state()` overwrite
it from `self.view` on every call, so nothing reads the stored copy. It stays because
`ViewState` is `gfxpanels`' public type and the panels match on `s.view` — the
alternative is a second parameter threaded through five functions in two modules to
carry one enum the struct already has a slot for.

- [ ] **Step 49: Convert the five accessors**

```rust
    /// Whether the box is drawn.
    pub const fn shown(&self) -> bool {
        self.on
    }

    /// Which view is shown, on either board.
    pub const fn view(&self) -> View {
        self.view
    }

    /// Everything CPS-1's views read.
    ///
    /// ⚠️ By value, not by reference: `view` is the viewer's field and the two
    /// states', so this composes the answer rather than pointing at a stored one. A
    /// `&ViewState` would have to point at a `view` that could be stale.
    pub const fn state(&self) -> ViewState {
        ViewState { view: self.view, ..self.cps1 }
    }

    /// Everything SF1's views read.
    pub const fn sf1_state(&self) -> Sf1ViewState {
        Sf1ViewState { view: self.view, ..self.sf1 }
    }

    /// The mask the loop must give a CPS-1's `Video`.
    ///
    /// Never enables anything: `LayerMask::all()` is the start and every toggle
    /// clears or restores one bit. The hardware's own `&&` in `Video::render` is what
    /// makes that structural — see `compose`'s `the_mask_can_only_subtract`.
    pub const fn mask(&self) -> LayerMask {
        self.cps1.mask
    }

    /// The mask the loop must give an `Sf1Video`.
    ///
    /// The same "subtracts only" guarantee, over SF1's own four fields — see
    /// `Plane::permitted` and `Sf1Video::render`'s `&&`.
    pub const fn sf1_mask(&self) -> Sf1LayerMask {
        self.sf1.mask
    }
```

⚠️ `..self.cps1` in a `const fn` requires the struct-update operand to be `Copy`,
which `ViewState` and `Sf1ViewState` both are. If a later field makes either
non-`Copy`, drop the `const` rather than reaching for a `Clone`.

- [ ] **Step 50: Convert `update`, `act`, `step`, `toggle_row` and `draw` to `&Machine`**

```rust
    /// Applies one frame's viewer keys. Returns whether the viewer is shown.
    ///
    /// `m` is read, never written: [`gfxpanels::map_origin`] and
    /// [`crate::sf1panels::map_origin`] read the scroll registers when a tilemap
    /// cursor leaves the beam, and a region's length bounds the tile view's paging.
    /// Nothing else.
    pub fn update(&mut self, a: &Actions, m: &Machine) -> bool {
        if a.gfx_toggled {
            self.on = !self.on;
        }
        // A hidden viewer ignores its own keys, so `]` does not silently page a view
        // you cannot see — and so the game's own `[`/`]` remain free. The mask is
        // *not* touched here: see this module's documentation.
        if !self.on {
            return false;
        }
        // The view cycles before the board is looked at, because it is the one piece
        // of state both boards share.
        if a.gfx_view_cycled {
            self.view = self.view.cycled();
        }
        if a.gfx_back {
            self.step(m, false);
        }
        if a.gfx_forward {
            self.step(m, true);
        }
        if a.gfx_act {
            self.act(m);
        }
        true
    }

    /// `Enter`: one key, four meanings per board, one per view.
    fn act(&mut self, m: &Machine) {
        match m {
            Machine::Cps1(_) => match self.view {
                View::Tiles => self.cps1.kind = next_kind(self.cps1.kind),
                View::Tilemap => {
                    // A new layer's tiles are a different size, so a cell index from
                    // the old one means nothing. Back to following the beam.
                    self.cps1.layer = next_layer(self.cps1.layer);
                    self.cps1.map_at = None;
                }
                // The palette's cursor is moved by `[`/`]` and there is nothing else
                // to act on — the view has one axis. Deliberately nothing, and named
                // so, because a silent `_ => {}` reads as an oversight.
                View::Palette => {}
                View::Layers => self.toggle_row(),
            },
            Machine::Sf1(_) => match self.view {
                // Four regions, not four tile sizes: each SF1 plane has a fixed
                // layout, so cycling the plane is what cycling the kind was.
                View::Tiles => self.sf1.plane = self.sf1.plane.cycled(),
                View::Tilemap => {
                    // A new map has different dimensions, so a cell from the old one
                    // means nothing — BG's (2000, 3) is not a cell of TX at all.
                    self.sf1.map = self.sf1.map.cycled();
                    self.sf1.map_at = None;
                }
                View::Palette => {}
                View::Layers => self.toggle_sf1_row(),
            },
        }
    }

    /// `[` and `]`: also one meaning per view, per board.
    fn step(&mut self, m: &Machine, forward: bool) {
        match m {
            Machine::Cps1(c) => match self.view {
                View::Tiles => {
                    let (cols, rows) = gfxpanels::tile_grid(self.cps1.kind);
                    self.cps1.tile_at = paged(self.cps1.tile_at, (cols * rows) as u32, forward);
                }
                View::Tilemap => {
                    // The first move materialises the cursor from wherever the beam
                    // is — the same "`None` is not a value" step `Debugger::scroll`
                    // takes.
                    let (c2, r) = self
                        .cps1
                        .map_at
                        .unwrap_or_else(|| gfxpanels::map_origin(c, self.cps1.layer));
                    // Wrapping, because the map itself wraps at 64: this is a
                    // coordinate in a torus, not an address.
                    let c2 = if forward {
                        (c2 + 1) % MAP_TILES
                    } else {
                        (c2 + MAP_TILES - 1) % MAP_TILES
                    };
                    self.cps1.map_at = Some((c2, r));
                }
                View::Palette => {
                    self.cps1.pal_at = if forward {
                        (self.cps1.pal_at + PAL_PAGE) % PENS
                    } else {
                        (self.cps1.pal_at + PENS - PAL_PAGE) % PENS
                    };
                }
                // Four rows, so both directions wrap — a selection that stuck at the
                // ends would need two keys to reach the row you can see is there.
                View::Layers => {
                    self.cps1.row = if forward {
                        (self.cps1.row + 1) % ROWS
                    } else {
                        (self.cps1.row + ROWS - 1) % ROWS
                    };
                }
            },
            Machine::Sf1(s) => match self.view {
                View::Tiles => {
                    let (cols, rows) = crate::sf1panels::tile_grid(self.sf1.plane);
                    self.sf1.tile_at = paged(self.sf1.tile_at, (cols * rows) as u32, forward);
                }
                View::Tilemap => {
                    // ⚠️ Each map's own width, not a shared constant: BG and FG are
                    // 2,048 columns and TX is 64. A `% MAP_TILES` here would wrap the
                    // background at 64 and make 31/32 of the map unreachable.
                    let cols = self.sf1.map.map().cols;
                    let (c2, r) = self
                        .sf1
                        .map_at
                        .unwrap_or_else(|| crate::sf1panels::map_origin(s, self.sf1.map));
                    let c2 = if forward {
                        (c2 + 1) % cols
                    } else {
                        (c2 + cols - 1 % cols) % cols
                    };
                    self.sf1.map_at = Some((c2, r));
                }
                View::Palette => {
                    self.sf1.pal_at = if forward {
                        (self.sf1.pal_at + PAL_PAGE) % SF1_PENS
                    } else {
                        (self.sf1.pal_at + SF1_PENS - PAL_PAGE) % SF1_PENS
                    };
                }
                View::Layers => {
                    self.sf1.row = if forward {
                        (self.sf1.row + 1) % ROWS
                    } else {
                        (self.sf1.row + ROWS - 1) % ROWS
                    };
                }
            },
        }
    }
```

Keep `toggle_row` exactly as it is, retargeted at `self.cps1`, and add SF1's beside
it:

```rust
    /// Subtracts, or restores, the selected row's layer, on CPS-1.
    ///
    /// Row 0 is the sprites, because that is `layer_order`'s value 0 and the layers
    /// view lists them in that order.
    fn toggle_row(&mut self) {
        let m = &mut self.cps1.mask;
        match self.cps1.row {
            0 => m.sprites = !m.sprites,
            1 => m.scroll1 = !m.scroll1,
            2 => m.scroll2 = !m.scroll2,
            // The row index comes from `step`, which is `% ROWS`, so this is row 3 and
            // not a fallback. Written as `_` because a `3 =>` arm would need an
            // unreachable one beside it, and that is worse.
            _ => m.scroll3 = !m.scroll3,
        }
    }

    /// Subtracts, or restores, the selected row's plane, on SF1.
    ///
    /// ⚠️ Row 0 is the *background*, not the sprites. SF1's drawing order is fixed at
    /// BG, FG, OB, TX, which is `Plane::ALL`'s order and the layers panel's row
    /// order; CPS-1's row 0 is the sprites because `layer_order`'s value 0 is. The
    /// two boards' row 0 mean different things and the panels label them.
    fn toggle_sf1_row(&mut self) {
        let m = &mut self.sf1.mask;
        match self.sf1.row {
            0 => m.bg = !m.bg,
            1 => m.fg = !m.fg,
            2 => m.sprites = !m.sprites,
            _ => m.tx = !m.tx,
        }
    }

    /// Draws the current view of whichever board this is, if shown.
    pub fn draw(&self, buf: &mut [u32], m: &Machine) {
        if !self.on {
            return;
        }
        match m {
            Machine::Cps1(c) => gfxpanels::draw(buf, c, &self.state()),
            Machine::Sf1(s) => crate::sf1panels::draw(buf, s, &self.sf1_state()),
        }
    }
```

And rewrite this module's fifth doc section, which says the opposite of the truth
now:

```rust
//! # Nothing here writes to the machine
//!
//! Every entry point takes `&Machine`. The viewer reads the scroll registers through
//! [`gfxpanels::map_origin`] or [`crate::sf1panels::map_origin`], and each board's
//! graphics regions through its own views, and that is all. `&Machine` rather than
//! `&Cps1` is also why there is no `as_cps1()` on `Machine`: a viewer that silently
//! did nothing on one board is a panel that goes blank with no error.
```

- [ ] **Step 51: Convert `gfx.rs`'s existing twenty-six tests**

The conversion is mechanical, and every one of these sites is in
`crates/frontend/src/gfx.rs`'s test module. Do them in this order; the compiler
finds every remaining one for you.

| What | Was | Becomes |
|------|-----|---------|
| The fixture, `:291` | `fn a_machine() -> Box<Cps1>` | `fn a_machine() -> Machine` — build the `Box<Cps1>` exactly as now, then `Machine::Cps1(m)` |
| `shown`, `:318` | `fn shown(m: &Cps1) -> GfxViewer` | `fn shown(m: &Machine) -> GfxViewer` |
| `looking_at`, `:326` | `fn looking_at(m: &Cps1, view: View)` | `fn looking_at(m: &Machine, view: View)` |
| Every `g.state().<field>` read (about forty) | unchanged text | unchanged — `state()` returns by value and field access still works |
| `g.state.map_at = …`, `:578` | `g.state.map_at` | `g.cps1.map_at` |
| `g.state.tile_at = …`, `:697` | `g.state.tile_at` | `g.cps1.tile_at` |
| `gfxpanels::map_origin(&m, …)`, `:554` and `:577` | `&m` is a `Box<Cps1>` deref | needs the inner `Cps1`: add the helper below and write `gfxpanels::map_origin(cps1(&m), …)` |
| `m.board.cps_a[…] = …`, `:551` | `&mut Box<Cps1>` | needs the inner `Cps1` mutably: `cps1_mut(&mut m).board.cps_a[…] = …` |
| `gfxpanels::draw(&mut expected, &m, g.state())`, `:832` | `g.state()` was `&ViewState` | `gfxpanels::draw(&mut expected, cps1(&m), &g.state())` |
| `&ViewState { pal_at: 0, ..*g.state() }`, `:843` | `..*g.state()` | `..g.state()` — no deref, it is a value now |
| `(m.total_cycles, m.cpu.pc, m.board.trace.acks)`, `:898` | direct fields | `let c = cps1(&m); (c.total_cycles, c.cpu.pc, c.board.trace.acks)` |

Add these two test-only helpers next to `a_machine`:

```rust
    /// The `Cps1` inside a `Machine` built by [`a_machine`].
    ///
    /// Test-only, and deliberately *not* a method on `Machine`: an
    /// `as_cps1() -> Option<&Cps1>` in the library would let production code write
    /// `if let Some(c) = m.as_cps1() { … }` and silently do nothing on SF1 — a panel
    /// that goes blank on one board with no error. Here the panic is the point.
    fn cps1(m: &Machine) -> &machine::Cps1 {
        match m {
            Machine::Cps1(c) => c,
            Machine::Sf1(_) => unreachable!("a_machine builds a Cps1"),
        }
    }

    /// Ditto, mutably, for the two tests that set a scroll register.
    fn cps1_mut(m: &mut Machine) -> &mut machine::Cps1 {
        match m {
            Machine::Cps1(c) => c,
            Machine::Sf1(_) => unreachable!("a_machine builds a Cps1"),
        }
    }
```

⚠️ Call `cps1`/`cps1_mut` per use, never bound once across a test body. A single
`let c = cps1_mut(&mut m);` held across a body holds `m` mutably, and the `&Machine`
the viewer needs is then rejected — E0502. This is the same trap Task 18's Step 6
documents for `loop_.rs`'s temporaries, for the same reason.

⚠️ These two helpers are **not** Task 21's to delete. Task 18's `cps1`/`cps1_ref` in
`loop_.rs` are temporary because production code must handle both boards; these are
in a test module whose fixture builds exactly one board, and a test that matched on
`Machine` in eight places to reach a field would be testing the enum.

```rust
    /// An `Sf1Video` with one palette entry set and one frame rendered.
    ///
    /// No ROM: the four graphics regions are empty, so `render` clears every pen to
    /// 0 and the whole frame is entry 0. That is enough to test the format, which is
    /// what this tests.
    fn an_sf1_frame() -> video::sf1::Sf1Video {
        let mut v = video::sf1::Sf1Video::new(Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new());
        let mut palette = vec![0u16; video::sf1::palette::ENTRIES];
        palette[0] = 0x0135;
        let videoram = vec![0u16; 0x800];
        let objectram = vec![0u16; 0x1000];
        v.render(&videoram, &objectram, &palette, 0, 0, 0);
        v
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_sf1_ppm_header_is_a_binary_p6_of_the_right_size() {
        let v = an_sf1_frame();
        let bytes = ppm_sf1(&v);
        let header = b"P6\n384 224\n255\n";
        assert_eq!(&bytes[..header.len()], header, "the exact header");
        assert_eq!(bytes.len() - header.len(), 258_048, "384 * 224 * 3 bytes of body");
        assert_eq!(bytes.len(), 258_063, "and nothing after it");
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_sf1_screenshot_uses_sf1s_dac_rule_and_not_cps1s() {
        let v = an_sf1_frame();
        let bytes = ppm_sf1(&v);
        let header = 15;
        // Entry 0x0135 through SF1's `(n << 4) | n` is (0x11, 0x33, 0x55). CPS-1's
        // converter would give (0x08, 0x18, 0x28) and the screenshot would be dark.
        assert_eq!(&bytes[header..header + 3], &[0x11, 0x33, 0x55]);
        assert_eq!(bytes[header..].chunks(3).all(|p| p == [0x11, 0x33, 0x55]), true);
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_never_rendered_sf1_screenshots_black_rather_than_panicking() {
        // `Framebuffer::new` fills every pen with CPS-1's 0xBFF, past SF1's 1,024
        // entries. `Sf1Video::rgb` answers black for it; indexing would not.
        let v = video::sf1::Sf1Video::new(Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new(), Vec::new());
        let bytes = ppm_sf1(&v);
        assert_eq!(bytes.len(), 258_063);
        assert!(bytes[15..].iter().all(|&b| b == 0));
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 57: Add `ppm_sf1` to `crates/sfemu/src/main.rs`**

Directly after `fn ppm`:

```rust
/// An SF1 frame as a binary PPM.
///
/// The same `P6\n384 224\n255\n` header as [`ppm`] — a screenshot of either board is
/// the same file format, and a viewer must not have to know which machine wrote it.
///
/// Separate from `ppm` and not a parameter, because there is no shared type to take:
/// `Video::rgb` returns an assembled `Vec<u8>` and `Sf1Video` publishes a per-pen
/// `rgb` instead. Assembling the body here rather than adding a frame-wide `rgb` to
/// `Sf1Video` keeps the allocation in the crate that is about to write a file, which
/// is the crate that already owns a filesystem.
fn ppm_sf1(v: &video::sf1::Sf1Video) -> Vec<u8> {
    let mut out = format!("P6\n{} {}\n255\n", video::WIDTH, video::HEIGHT).into_bytes();
    assert_eq!(
        v.fb.pens.len(),
        video::WIDTH * video::HEIGHT,
        "one pen per pixel of the visible frame"
    );
    for &pen in v.fb.pens.iter() {
        // `rgb` and not a palette index: a never-rendered frame holds CPS-1's
        // `BACKGROUND_PEN`, which is past SF1's 1,024 entries, and `rgb` answers
        // black for it where an index would be out of bounds.
        out.extend_from_slice(&v.rgb(pen));
    }
    out
}
```

⚠️ `ppm_sf1` is dead code until Task 21 wires `screenshot` to it, so this step's
commit will not pass `-D warnings` on its own. Add `#[allow(dead_code)]` above it
with the comment `// Wired to `screenshot` in the loop's board fork.` and delete the
attribute in Task 21 — a plan that left it out would have the implementer discover
the warning and reach for something worse.

**Verification, for a reviewer**

The task is done when: `tilemap::sample` is the single source of the raster mapping and
`draw` calls it; `Plane` and `MapKind` name every per-plane and per-map decision, and
`Sf1Video::render` reads them rather than repeating them inline; `Sf1Video` publishes
`region`, `gfx` and `tilerom`, and the tile browser's in-region test is
`code < gfx.elements()` with no `tile_in_rom` analogue; `sf1panels.rs` draws four
views for an `&Sf1` using `gfxpanels`' chrome without a third copy of `PAD`, with
four-character tilemap row labels and a panel line naming the tilemap ROM as the
background's source; `GfxViewer` holds one `view` and two states, takes `&Machine` at
every entry point, and returns both states by value; `pens_to_argb_sf1` and `ppm_sf1`
use SF1's `(n << 4) | n` expansion and agree with `sf1::palette::entry_to_rgb` over
all 65,536 entries; and the four vector suites are unchanged.

⚠️ What is **not** in this task, and must not be added to it: any change to
`crates/sfemu/src/loop_.rs` (Task 21 — `gfx.update`/`gfx.draw` now take `&Machine`,
which is exactly why the loop goes last), the deletion of Task 18's `cps1`/`cps1_ref`
temporaries in `loop_.rs` (Task 21), `LoopOpts::board` (Task 21), and wiring
`ppm_sf1` into `screenshot` (Task 21). The `#[allow(dead_code)]` on `ppm_sf1` is the
marker for that last one. This task makes both boards' viewers exist and proves each
draws its own board; the loop choosing between them is the loop's.

---

**Why this task is last, and what it is allowed to be big about**

Task 20's closing ⚠️ names this task four times and Task 18's names it five, from the
other side. Both lists agree, which is the check that the boundary is real:

> Task 21 converts the loop's eight remaining `&Cps1` helpers, deletes `cps1`,
> `cps1_ref` and both `view()` twins, moves `const BOARD` into `LoopOpts`, and replaces
> `FramePacer::cps1()` with `FramePacer::new(m.frame_ns(), MAX_CATCH_UP)`.

The loop is last because every one of those eight helpers reaches something an earlier
task had to build first: three of them are `gfx::update`/`step`/`draw` (Task 20), two are
`save`/`load` (Task 19), one is the sound panel through `Debugger::draw` (Task 18), and
one is `pens_to_argb` (Task 20). Converting them in any earlier task would mean either a
half-converted loop that does not compile or a second set of temporaries to delete later.

⚠️ **This task is large because it is one signature change.** `run(m: &mut Cps1, …)`
becoming `run(m: &mut Machine, …)` touches 52 test call sites, three fixtures and about
130 field reads in `loop_.rs`'s test module. That is a lot of lines and almost no
decisions: the decisions are the nine places production code has to *choose* an arm, and
those are listed in Step 12's table. Split differently, a reviewer would be asked to
approve a loop that cannot be run.

**The nine board decisions in the loop, and nothing else**

Everything else in `run` goes through `Machine`'s dispatching methods unchanged. These
nine are the places where the two boards genuinely differ, and each one is a `match`
rather than an accessor for the reason `machine.rs`'s module doc gives — an
`as_cps1() -> Option<&Cps1>` would let a caller write an `if let` that silently does
nothing on the other board:

| # | What | CPS-1 | SF1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The pacer's period | 16,768,000 ns | 16,666,667 ns — through `m.frame_ns()`, not a match |
| 2 | The inputs | `c.board.inputs = a.inputs` | `f.board.inputs = Sf1Inputs::from_shared(&a.inputs)` |
| 3 | The layer mask | `c.video.enable = gfx.mask()` | `f.video.enable = gfx.sf1_mask()` |
| 4 | The host ring's counters | `c.sound.set_audio_stats(..)` | `f.set_audio_stats(..)` |
| 5 | Pens to ARGB | `pens_to_argb(&c.video, ..)` | `pens_to_argb_sf1(&f.video, ..)` |
| 6 | Save | `encode(&c.snapshot(), o.board)` | `encode_sf1(&f.snapshot())` |
| 7 | Load | `decode(&bytes, o.board)` → `c.restore` | `decode_sf1(&bytes)` → `f.restore` |
| 8 | Screenshot | `crate::ppm(&c.video)` | `crate::ppm_sf1(&f.video)` |
| 9 | The state tag `main` recorded | `BOARD_SF2` | `BOARD_SF1` |

⚠️ **Number 4 is not symmetric and that is not a mistake.** CPS-1's counters live on the
`SoundBoard` (`sound.rs:337`), because CPS-1 has one sound board and the ring's numbers
sit beside the OKI's clip count on the same struct. SF1 has *two* sound boards and the
ring belongs to neither, so Step 1 puts them on `Sf1` itself. A reader who expects
`f.fm.set_audio_stats(..)` by analogy will not find it, which is why the table says so.

**Why `LoopOpts::board` is read on one arm**

Spec item 16 requires it: "`loop_.rs`'s `BOARD` const is a `LoopOpts` field." It is read
by number 6 and number 7 above, both on the CPS-1 arm only, because Task 19's
`encode_sf1`/`decode_sf1` bake `BOARD_SF1` in — SF1's payload length is validated in the
same call and there is exactly one board that length belongs to.

A field one arm ignores can drift from the machine beside it, so the loop does not let
it: `state_tag` is one `const fn` with two callers — the `debug_assert_eq!` at the top of
`run` and `main`'s `loop_opts` — and it is what makes the pairing checkable rather than
conventional. `debug_assert_eq!` and not `assert_eq!` because a wrong pairing is a bug in
`main` that no user input can cause, and a panic in the loop would take the window with
it in a release build.

**What must not be re-widened**

⚠️ **The host audio path is already interleaved stereo.** Task 14 did that: `CHANNELS`
is 2, `Resampler::feed`'s slices are interleaved, `Ring`'s `capacity`/`prefill`/`len` are
in frames and `len_samples` is the sample count. `Machine::samples()`'s doc says both
boards produce two channels. Nothing in this task touches channel width, and a diff here
that mentions `CHANNELS` outside a frame-count division is the signal that something has
been re-derived.

⚠️ **`SAMPLE_RATE_NUM`/`SAMPLE_RATE_DEN` do not fork.** `audio.rs:15-17` is
`SOUND_XTAL / YM_SAMPLE_CLOCKS` — 3,579,545 / 64 = 55,930 Hz — and SF1's `sample_acc`
accrues against the same `YM_SAMPLE_CLOCKS`, so the rate `open_audio` prints is right on
both boards. `open_audio` is not in this task's diff.

# This crate holds no ROM

No ROM is bundled, fetched, or committed, including as a test fixture. Every test in
this task builds its board from a program written inline — the loop's fixtures are the
five-instruction diverging counters they already are, and the SF1 fixture is Task 18's
`vec![0x18, 0xFE]` (`jr -2`) and `vec![0x00, 0x18, 0xFE]`. The three new integration
tests are the *only* files in this task that need real ROM data, they are gated on
`SFEMU_ROMS` exactly as their three CPS-1 siblings are, and each panics with a message
naming that one variable and the set it wants. No second escape hatch, no URL, and
`/testdata` gains nothing.

⚠️ **`SFEMU_ROMS` holds one path, and the six gated tests do not all want the same one.**
The three CPS-1 tests want an `sf2` set and the three new ones want an `sf` set, so a
user who has both runs the suite twice with the variable pointing at each in turn. That
is deliberate: a second variable — `SFEMU_ROMS_SF1` — is the second escape hatch this
project's rule forbids by name, and the failure when the variable points at the wrong set
is a `romset::load` error naming the file it could not find, which is loud.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test for `Sf1`'s host-ring counters**

Append to the `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` at the foot of `crates/machine/src/sf1/machine.rs`.

⚠️ **Two assertions with two different numbers, and that is the point.** `sound.rs:887`
records that swapping the two assignments inside `SoundBoard::set_audio_stats` left all
156 tests of that file passing, because every test that touched it passed equal values or
asserted only one of them. 17 and 4 are chosen to be unequal, and both are read back.

```rust
    /// The host's ring statistics arrive from outside and are readable.
    ///
    /// # Why these live on `Sf1` and not on a sound board
    ///
    /// CPS-1 keeps them on [`crate::sound::SoundBoard`], where they sit beside the
    /// OKI's clip count, because CPS-1 has one sound board. SF1 has two — the FM
    /// board and the ADPCM board — and the host's ring belongs to neither: it is
    /// downstream of the *mix*. Putting them on `Sf1` is the only placement that
    /// does not make one of the two boards arbitrarily the owner.
    ///
    /// # Why two unequal numbers
    ///
    /// `sound.rs:887` records that swapping the two assignments in CPS-1's version
    /// of this setter left 156 tests passing. Equal values, or reading back only
    /// one, is what let that mutant live.
    #[test]
    fn the_host_ring_counters_arrive_from_outside_and_read_back_apart() {
        let mut m = a_machine();
        assert_eq!(m.audio_drops(), 0, "a fresh machine has dropped nothing");
        assert_eq!(m.audio_underruns(), 0, "and starved nothing");
        m.set_audio_stats(17, 4);
        assert_eq!(m.audio_drops(), 17);
        assert_eq!(m.audio_underruns(), 4);
    }

    /// Running the machine does not disturb them, because it does not own them.
    ///
    /// The counters describe the host's ring, and `run_frame` cannot see the host.
    /// A `+= 1` written into the sample path by mistake would climb here.
    #[test]
    fn running_does_not_change_the_host_ring_counters() {
        let mut m = a_machine();
        m.set_audio_stats(17, 4);
        m.run_frame();
        assert_eq!((m.audio_drops(), m.audio_underruns()), (17, 4));
    }

    /// The saturating helper covers them, so the panel's width test covers them too.
    ///
    /// `no_sf1_row_overflows_its_box_with_every_counter_saturated` asserts nothing
    /// about *which* counters `saturate_counters_for_test` sets; it draws the panel
    /// and looks for ink outside the box. A counter the helper misses is a column
    /// that test silently never widens. This is the assertion that makes the
    /// helper's coverage of these two checkable here, where the fields live.
    #[test]
    fn saturating_covers_the_host_ring_counters() {
        let mut m = a_machine();
        m.saturate_counters_for_test();
        assert_eq!(m.audio_drops(), u32::MAX);
        assert_eq!(m.audio_underruns(), u32::MAX);
    }
```

⚠️ `a_machine()` is Task 15's fixture in this same module. Do not write a new one, and do
not reach for Task 18's `an_sf1_machine()` — that one lives in `frontend` and returns a
`Machine`.


Then, in `impl Sf1`, beside the other counter accessors:

```rust
    /// Record the host audio ring's counters, so the debug panel can show them.
    ///
    /// The ring lives in `sfemu` — it is sized from the host's sample rate, which
    /// `machine` has no business knowing — so these arrive from outside rather than
    /// being counted here. This is [`crate::sound::SoundBoard::set_audio_stats`]'s
    /// job, moved up a level: CPS-1 has one sound board to hang them on and SF1 has
    /// two, so on this board they belong to the machine.
    pub fn set_audio_stats(&mut self, drops: u32, underruns: u32) {
        self.audio_drops = drops;
        self.audio_underruns = underruns;
    }

    /// Frames the host's ring dropped. See [`Sf1::set_audio_stats`].
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn audio_drops(&self) -> u32 {
        self.audio_drops
    }

    /// Times the host's ring ran dry. See [`Sf1::set_audio_stats`].
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn audio_underruns(&self) -> u32 {
        self.audio_underruns
    }
```

And in `saturate_counters_for_test`, beside the existing five, adding to the same
assignment block:

```rust
        self.audio_drops = u32::MAX;
        self.audio_underruns = u32::MAX;
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Write the failing test for the panel's `DRP`/`UND` segment**

Task 18's `draw_sf1` prints no `DRP`/`UND` where CPS-1 does, and its ⚠️ says why:

> ⚠️ **No `DRP`/`UND` here, unlike CPS-1.** Those two are the *host's* ring statistics,
> they arrive through `SoundBoard::set_audio_stats` and `Sf1` has no equivalent door.
> Printing two hardcoded zeroes would read as "the host is fine" on a board that has
> never been asked. Task 21 adds the door with the loop.

Step 3 added the door. Now the panel asks.

**Where it goes, and why there is only one place it can.** The box is at its ceiling in
both directions:

- **Rows.** `SND_ROWS` is 17, and `SND_Y + 17 * LINE + 2 * PAD` is 211 against
  `STATUS_Y` 214. 18 rows is 218, which is under the status line. Task 18's ⚠️ is
  explicit: "Do not raise `SND_ROWS` and do not move `STATUS_Y`." So this is not a row.
- **Columns.** `SF1_COLS` is 45 and row 12 (`DSC`/`OVR`/`UNM`) is 44 of them. Task 18's
  other ⚠️: "Do not add a field to row 12."
- **The one row with room** is row 14, `FET {:010} YM {:010}`, at 28 characters. Task 18
  measured the space it leaves: "one `DRP {:010}` fits at 43, two do not at 57."

So one column on row 14, and `UND` cannot join it there. It goes on **row 4**, `KEYON
01234567`, which is 14 characters — the widest slack in the panel — giving
`KEYON 01234567 UND 4294967295` at **29**.

⚠️ **The two are on different rows and that is not tidy.** It is what the box allows. A
reader looking for the pair will find `DRP` beside the fetch counters, where the other
host-facing numbers are, and `UND` beside the key-on mask. The alternative is a 46-column
box, which is past `WIDTH` 384 — `SND_X + 46 * ADVANCE + 2 * PAD` is 388.

Append to `crates/frontend/src/sndpanel.rs`'s test module:

```rust
    /// The SF1 panel prints the host ring's counters, and prints the machine's.
    ///
    /// # Why a rendered-text assertion and not a call count
    ///
    /// The failure this catches is two hardcoded zeroes — the thing Task 18's ⚠️
    /// forbade until the door existed. A test that asserted `draw_sf1` reads
    /// `m.audio_drops()` would pass on a panel that read it and printed a literal.
    /// So the numbers go in through the door and come out of the glyphs.
    #[test]
    fn the_sf1_panel_shows_the_host_rings_counters() {
        let mut m = an_sf1_machine();
        as_sf1(&mut m).set_audio_stats(1_234, 56);
        let mut buf = vec![0u32; WIDTH * HEIGHT];
        draw(&mut buf, &m);
        let text = text_in_box(&buf);
        assert!(
            text.contains("DRP 0000001234"),
            "the fetch row must carry the drop count: {text}"
        );
        assert!(
            text.contains("UND 0000000056"),
            "and the key-on row the underrun count: {text}"
        );
    }

    /// Zero is printed as zero, and that now means something.
    ///
    /// Before the door existed, a zero here would have been a lie about a board
    /// nobody had asked. It is now a measurement: the loop calls
    /// `set_audio_stats` every frame, so a zero means the ring is keeping up.
    #[test]
    fn an_untroubled_host_ring_reads_zero_on_the_sf1_panel() {
        let m = an_sf1_machine();
        let mut buf = vec![0u32; WIDTH * HEIGHT];
        draw(&mut buf, &m);
        let text = text_in_box(&buf);
        assert!(text.contains("DRP 0000000000"), "{text}");
        assert!(text.contains("UND 0000000000"), "{text}");
    }
```

⚠️ **`text_in_box` does not exist and this task does not add it.** Replace both
`text_in_box(&buf)` calls with the check Task 18's tests already use for this file — they
assert on ink positions, not on decoded glyphs, because there is no OCR here. Concretely:
draw twice, once with `set_audio_stats(0, 0)` and once with `set_audio_stats(1_234, 56)`,
and assert the two buffers **differ**, then assert the difference lies inside the box:

```rust
    #[test]
    fn the_sf1_panel_shows_the_host_rings_counters() {
        let mut a = vec![0u32; WIDTH * HEIGHT];
        let mut b = vec![0u32; WIDTH * HEIGHT];
        let mut m = an_sf1_machine();
        as_sf1(&mut m).set_audio_stats(0, 0);
        draw(&mut a, &m);
        as_sf1(&mut m).set_audio_stats(1_234, 56);
        draw(&mut b, &m);
        assert_ne!(
            a, b,
            "the panel does not read the host ring's counters at all — two \
             hardcoded zeroes render identically whatever the machine says"
        );
        // And the change is on the two rows it should be on, not anywhere else.
        // Rows 4 and 14 of the header, in the panel's own coordinates.
        for (row, what) in [(4usize, "UND"), (14usize, "DRP")] {
            let y0 = SND_Y + PAD + row * LINE;
            let changed = (y0..y0 + LINE).any(|y| {
                (0..WIDTH).any(|x| a[y * WIDTH + x] != b[y * WIDTH + x])
            });
            assert!(changed, "row {row} carries {what} and did not change");
        }
        let rows: Vec<usize> = (0..SND_ROWS)
            .filter(|&r| {
                let y0 = SND_Y + PAD + r * LINE;
                (y0..y0 + LINE).any(|y| (0..WIDTH).any(|x| a[y * WIDTH + x] != b[y * WIDTH + x]))
            })
            .collect();
        assert_eq!(rows, vec![4, 14], "only those two rows may move");
    }
```

⚠️ **The second test above goes away with `text_in_box`.** "Zero renders as zero" is not
observable without decoding glyphs, and the `assert_ne!` pair above is the assertion that
actually carries the requirement. Write only the one test.

⚠️ `SND_ROWS`, `SND_Y`, `PAD` and `LINE` must be in scope in the test module — `PAD` and
`LINE` come from `crate::overlay::PAD` and `crate::font::LINE`, which the file's
production `use` list already imports (Task 18's Interfaces made `overlay::PAD`
`pub(crate)` for exactly this).

- [ ] **Step 8: Add the two columns**

In `crates/frontend/src/sndpanel.rs`'s `draw_sf1`, row 4 becomes:

```rust
    // `UND` shares this row because it is the widest slack in the panel: `KEYON`
    // plus eight channel digits is 14 of 45 columns. Its partner `DRP` is eleven
    // rows down on the fetch row — the pair does not fit on either row alone, and
    // the box cannot grow: 46 columns is 388 pixels of a 384-pixel window.
    line(buf, row, HI, &format!(
        "KEYON {:08b} UND {:010}",
        m.fm.keyon_mask(),
        m.audio_underruns()
    ));
    row += 1;
```

and row 14 becomes:

```rust
    // `DRP` uses the 17 columns row 14 has spare. Its partner `UND` is on the
    // key-on row; see there.
    line(buf, row, FG, &format!(
        "FET {:010} YM {:010} DRP {:010}",
        m.fm.trace().audiocpu_fetches,
        m.fm.trace().ym_writes,
        m.audio_drops()
    ));
    row += 1;
```

⚠️ **`{:010}` and not `{:06}`.** Task 18's ⚠️ says why: `{:06}` is a *minimum* width, so a
saturated `u32` prints ten digits in a field measured for six and walks out of the box.
Both new columns are `u32`, both are `{:010}`, and both widths are in the measurements
above: 29 and 43, against 45.

⚠️ **`keyon_mask()` and `trace()` are placeholders for whatever Task 18's row 4 and row 14
actually call.** Do not change those expressions — copy the existing rows and add only the
new segment. If the row you find does not match the text above verbatim, the row you found
is right and this snippet is stale.

- [ ] **Step 11: Write the failing test for `Sf1Inputs::from_shared`**

The keyboard produces `machine::Inputs` — CPS-1's shape. SF1's board reads `Sf1Inputs`.
Nothing in Tasks 1–20 converts between them, and the spec says who does:

> `machine::inputs`'s `in0()`/`in1()`/`in2()` bit packing (`inputs.rs:90-114`) is CPS-1's
> and does not transfer; its **doctrine** does — active low, `Default` = `idle()` rather
> than all-zero, `PlayerInput` as a struct of named booleans. F keeps the struct and
> replaces the packers.

**Where the conversion lives, and why not in `frontend`.** `keys.rs`'s module doc forbids
`frontend` from computing port values:

> **This module does not know about polarity** — Every field of [`machine::Inputs`] is
> `true` for *pressed*, and `machine` does the active-low conversion in one place. This
> module sets booleans and nothing else. Computing port values here would duplicate the
> project's only piece of polarity logic.

A conversion from one boolean struct to another is not polarity logic, but it *is*
knowledge of which of SF1's four ports each button lands on — `service`, `start1` and
`start2` move from `IN0` to `SYSTEM`, and two of six attack buttons move from `IN1` to
`IN0`. That is the same knowledge `keys.rs` is forbidden to hold, and `machine::sf1::inputs`
already holds all of it. So the conversion is a constructor there.

**What crosses and what does not:**

| `Inputs` field | `Sf1Inputs` | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `coin1`, `coin2` | copied | Both boards have two coin slots |
| `service` | copied | Moves port — `IN0` bit 5 on CPS-1, `SYSTEM` 0x0004 here — but the packer handles that |
| `start1`, `start2` | copied | Likewise: `IN0` there, `SYSTEM` here |
| `p1`, `p2` | copied | ⚠️ `PlayerInput` is the *same type*, shared verbatim. Task 8 says so. |
| `test` | **dropped** | SF1 has no `PORT_SERVICE` bit. Service mode is `DSW1_SERVICE_MODE`, a switch, not a button |
| `dsw: [u8; 3]` | **not carried** | Three 8-bit ports against two 16-bit ones, with no correspondence between any pair. `Sf1Inputs::idle()`'s defaults stand |

⚠️ **`dsw` is where a plausible conversion does damage.** `Sf1Inputs`'s `DSW1_DEFAULT` is
`0xFFFF & !DSW1_DEMO_SOUNDS`, because on this board **clear means on** for Demo_Sounds —
the spec calls this out as the one setting whose default is the opposite polarity to every
other in the table, and says that a "default = all bits set" shortcut "silently mutes the
attract mode". Copying CPS-1's `[0xFF; 3]` into SF1's `[u16; 2]` by widening produces
exactly `[0xFFFF, 0xFFFF]` and exactly that silent mute. So the constructor starts from
`idle()` and never touches `dsw`, and a test pins that.

⚠️ **`DSW1_DEFAULT` and `DSW2_DEFAULT` are private to `sf1/inputs.rs`** (Task 8 made them
so deliberately). The test below cannot name them and must not — it asserts against
`Sf1Inputs::idle()`'s *observable* output, which is the same check with no access to the
constant the code reads. That is this project's "every expected value is a literal" rule:
a test that read `DSW1_DEFAULT` to check `from_shared` copied `DSW1_DEFAULT` would prove
nothing.

Append to `crates/machine/src/sf1/inputs.rs`'s test module:

```rust
    /// Every pressed button crosses from the shared struct, one at a time.
    ///
    /// # Why one field per iteration and not one struct with everything set
    ///
    /// A `from_shared` that ignored `coin2` and copied `coin1` twice passes a test
    /// that presses everything and checks the ports are not idle. Each iteration
    /// here presses exactly one thing and asserts exactly which bit moved, so a
    /// crossed wire between any two of the five names fails on both of them.
    #[test]
    fn each_shared_button_reaches_its_own_sf1_bit() {
        // (name, the setter, the port to read, the bit that must clear)
        // Every value is a literal from `sf.cpp`'s `sfus` port block, not read from
        // this module's constants: SYSTEM's START1/START2/SERVICE1 at 0x0001/0x0002/
        // 0x0004 (`sf.cpp:701-703`) and IN0's COIN1/COIN2 at 0x0001/0x0002.
        let cases: [(&str, fn(&mut crate::inputs::Inputs), fn(&Sf1Inputs) -> u16, u16); 5] = [
            ("coin1", |i| i.coin1 = true, |s| s.in0(), 0x0001),
            ("coin2", |i| i.coin2 = true, |s| s.in0(), 0x0002),
            ("start1", |i| i.start1 = true, |s| s.system(), 0x0001),
            ("start2", |i| i.start2 = true, |s| s.system(), 0x0002),
            ("service", |i| i.service = true, |s| s.system(), 0x0004),
        ];
        for (name, press, port, bit) in cases {
            let mut shared = crate::inputs::Inputs::idle();
            press(&mut shared);
            let idle = Sf1Inputs::idle();
            let got = Sf1Inputs::from_shared(&shared);
            assert_eq!(
                port(&idle) & !port(&got),
                bit,
                "{name} must clear exactly {bit:#06X}: idle {:#06X}, pressed {:#06X}",
                port(&idle),
                port(&got)
            );
        }
    }

    /// The stick and all six attack buttons cross, on both players.
    ///
    /// `PlayerInput` is the same type on both boards — Task 8 keeps it verbatim —
    /// so this is a move, not a translation. What it catches is a `from_shared` that
    /// forgot `p2`, or one that assigned `p1` to both.
    #[test]
    fn both_players_sticks_and_buttons_cross_whole() {
        let mut shared = crate::inputs::Inputs::idle();
        shared.p1.left = true;
        shared.p1.punch = [true, true, true];
        shared.p2.up = true;
        shared.p2.kick = [true, true, true];
        let got = Sf1Inputs::from_shared(&shared);
        // Read back through the fields rather than the ports: the ports are
        // `in0()`/`in1()`'s business and Task 8's tests already pin their packing.
        // What is under test here is that the struct arrived intact.
        assert!(got.p1.left, "p1 left");
        assert_eq!(got.p1.punch, [true, true, true], "p1 punches");
        assert!(got.p2.up, "p2 up");
        assert_eq!(got.p2.kick, [true, true, true], "p2 kicks");
        assert!(!got.p1.up, "and nothing p1 did not press");
        assert!(!got.p2.left, "nor p2");
    }

    /// The DIP switches do not cross, and the attract music survives.
    ///
    /// # The trap this exists for
    ///
    /// `Inputs::dsw` is `[0xFF; 3]` when idle. `Sf1Inputs::dsw` is `[u16; 2]` and
    /// its first entry is *not* all ones: Demo_Sounds is 0x2000 and on this board a
    /// **clear** bit means on, which is the opposite polarity to every other default
    /// in `sf.cpp`'s table. A conversion that widened CPS-1's bytes — or that reached
    /// for a "default = all bits set" shortcut — would set 0x2000 and silently mute
    /// the attract mode.
    ///
    /// This asserts against `idle()`'s output rather than against `DSW1_DEFAULT`,
    /// which is private and is the constant the code reads: a test that read it
    /// would be checking the code against itself.
    #[test]
    fn the_dip_switches_keep_their_own_defaults() {
        let mut shared = crate::inputs::Inputs::idle();
        shared.dsw = [0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF];
        shared.test = true; // and this has nowhere to go on SF1 — see below.
        let got = Sf1Inputs::from_shared(&shared);
        assert_eq!(
            got.dsw,
            Sf1Inputs::idle().dsw,
            "the shared struct's three bytes must not reach these two words"
        );
        // Demo sounds are on, which is what a clear 0x2000 means. Stated as the
        // literal from `sf.cpp`'s table and not as `DSW1_DEMO_SOUNDS`.
        assert_eq!(got.dsw[0] & 0x2000, 0, "attract music must not be muted");
        // Service mode is off, which is what a *set* 0x8000 means on the same port.
        assert_eq!(got.dsw[0] & 0x8000, 0x8000, "service mode must be off");
    }

    /// A wholly idle shared struct converts to a wholly idle SF1 one.
    ///
    /// The floor under the three tests above: if `from_shared` set a bit nobody
    /// pressed, one of them would still pass and this one would not.
    #[test]
    fn idle_converts_to_idle() {
        let got = Sf1Inputs::from_shared(&crate::inputs::Inputs::idle());
        let want = Sf1Inputs::idle();
        // The four ports, as literals: `in0()` and `in1()` all ones, `system()`
        // 0xFF7F because `sf.cpp:709` makes bit 7 active *high*.
        assert_eq!((got.in0(), got.in1()), (0xFFFF, 0xFFFF));
        assert_eq!(got.system(), 0xFF7F);
        assert_eq!(
            (got.in0(), got.in1(), got.system()),
            (want.in0(), want.in1(), want.system())
        );
    }
```

⚠️ **`Inputs::idle()` and not `Inputs::default()`** in every case above, even though
`Default` delegates to it. `idle()` names the thing being relied on; `default()` would
leave a reader to check.

- [ ] **Step 13: Write `from_shared`**

In `crates/machine/src/sf1/inputs.rs`, in `impl Sf1Inputs`, after `idle()`:

```rust
    /// The shared [`crate::inputs::Inputs`] as this board's ports see it.
    ///
    /// # Why the conversion is here and not in `frontend`
    ///
    /// The keyboard produces the shared struct — one boolean per key, `true` for
    /// pressed, and `frontend/src/keys.rs`'s module doc forbids that crate from
    /// knowing anything more. What this function knows is which of SF1's four ports
    /// each name lands on, and three of them land somewhere different from CPS-1:
    /// `service`, `start1` and `start2` are on `SYSTEM` here where CPS-1 has them on
    /// `IN0`, and two of the six attack buttons are on `IN0` here where CPS-1 has all
    /// six across `IN1` and `IN2`. That knowledge is this module's, so the conversion
    /// is too.
    ///
    /// # What does not cross
    ///
    /// - **`dsw`.** Three 8-bit ports against two 16-bit ones, with no pair
    ///   corresponding. This starts from [`Sf1Inputs::idle`] and leaves the switches
    ///   alone, which matters more than it looks: Demo_Sounds is the one setting in
    ///   `sf.cpp`'s table whose default is a *clear* bit, so anything that widened
    ///   `[0xFF; 3]` into `[0xFFFF; 2]` would mute the attract mode and say nothing.
    /// - **`test`.** CPS-1's `PORT_SERVICE` bit on `IN0`. This board has no such
    ///   button: service mode is `DSW1_SERVICE_MODE`, a switch on `DSW1`, and a
    ///   momentary keypress is not what one of those is.
    ///
    /// `p1` and `p2` cross whole, because [`crate::inputs::PlayerInput`] is one type
    /// shared by both boards — six attack buttons and a four-way stick is six attack
    /// buttons and a four-way stick, whatever the wiring does with them.
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn from_shared(i: &crate::inputs::Inputs) -> Self {
        Self {
            coin1: i.coin1,
            coin2: i.coin2,
            service: i.service,
            start1: i.start1,
            start2: i.start2,
            p1: i.p1,
            p2: i.p2,
            // Not `i.dsw`: see the note above on Demo_Sounds. `idle()` is `const`,
            // so this is the switch defaults with no runtime cost and no second
            // copy of the two constants.
            dsw: Self::idle().dsw,
        }
    }
```

⚠️ **`const fn` is possible here and is worth having.** Every field is `Copy`, `idle()` is
`pub const fn`, and `PlayerInput` is a plain `Copy` struct. If the compiler rejects it, drop
the `const` — but check the reason first, because `Self::idle().dsw` in a `const fn` is the
only construct here that could fail and it is also the one carrying the Demo_Sounds
correctness.

```bash
The keyboard produces `machine::Inputs` — one boolean per key, CPS-1's shape.
This board reads `Sf1Inputs`. Nothing converted between them.

The conversion is here rather than in `frontend` because of what it knows:
```

- [ ] **Step 16: Write the failing test for `LoopOpts::board` and `state_tag`**

`const BOARD: u32 = frontend::BOARD_SF2;` is what makes the loop CPS-1-only. Spec item 16
says where it goes: "`loop_.rs`'s `BOARD` const is a `LoopOpts` field."

⚠️ **A field one arm never reads can drift from the machine beside it.** `encode_sf1` and
`decode_sf1` take no board argument — Task 19 baked `BOARD_SF1` in — so `o.board` is read
only on the CPS-1 arm. That leaves a `LoopOpts { board: BOARD_SF2 }` beside a
`Machine::Sf1` as a thing that compiles, runs, and is wrong in a way nothing surfaces. So
the pairing is checkable: one `const fn` with two callers, and a `debug_assert_eq!` at the
top of `run`.

`debug_assert_eq!` and not `assert_eq!`: a wrong pairing is a bug in `main`, not something
a user's input can cause, and a `panic!` inside `run` takes the window down mid-game in a
release build.

Append to `crates/sfemu/src/loop_.rs`'s test module:

```rust
    /// Each board's tag is the one its codec writes.
    ///
    /// Every value is a literal here, not `frontend::BOARD_SF2`: this is the mapping
    /// under test and reading the constant the function returns would prove nothing.
    /// The bytes are the format's documented form — big-endian ASCII, so a hex dump
    /// of a state file reads `SF2\0` or `SF1\0`.
    #[test]
    fn each_board_has_its_own_state_tag() {
        assert_eq!(state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Cps1), 0x5346_3200);
        assert_eq!(state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Sf1), 0x5346_3100);
        assert_eq!(&state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Cps1).to_be_bytes(), b"SF2\0");
        assert_eq!(&state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Sf1).to_be_bytes(), b"SF1\0");
        assert_ne!(
            state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Cps1),
            state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Sf1),
            "two boards sharing a tag is the whole failure the tag exists to stop"
        );
    }

    /// The tag `state_tag` answers is the tag the codec checks.
    ///
    /// # Why this is not the same test as the one above
    ///
    /// The one above pins `state_tag` against two literals. This pins it against
    /// `frontend`'s codec, which is the thing that has to agree: a `state_tag` that
    /// returned the right-looking number while `frontend::BOARD_SF2` was something
    /// else would pass up there and fail here.
    #[test]
    fn the_state_tag_is_the_one_the_codec_accepts() {
        let mut m = machine();
        m.run_frame();
        let bytes = frontend::encode(
            &cps1(&m).snapshot(),
            state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Cps1),
        );
        assert!(
            frontend::decode(&bytes, state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Cps1)).is_ok(),
            "a state written with this tag must decode under it"
        );
        assert!(
            matches!(
                frontend::decode(&bytes, state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Sf1)),
                Err(frontend::StateError::WrongBoard { .. })
            ),
            "and must be refused under the other board's"
        );
    }
```

⚠️ **`cps1(&m)` in the second test is the test-module helper Step 19 adds**, not Task 18's
production temporary — that one is deleted in this same task. See Step 19's ⚠️.

```rust

    ///
    /// # Why a field and not derived from the machine
    ///
    /// It could be — `state_tag(m.board())` is available everywhere it is read. It is
    /// a field because it is a *session* fact that `main` establishes when it picks a
    /// board, and because [`run`]'s `debug_assert_eq!` can then check `main`'s answer
    /// against the machine it was handed. Deriving it would make the two agree by
    /// construction and check nothing.
    ///
    /// ⚠️ **Read on the CPS-1 arm only.** [`frontend::encode_sf1`] and
    /// [`frontend::decode_sf1`] take no board argument: Task 19 baked `BOARD_SF1`
    /// into them, because SF1's payload length is validated in the same call and
    /// there is exactly one board that length belongs to. So this field is what
    /// CPS-1's `encode`/`decode` are given, and on an SF1 session it is checked by
    /// the assertion and otherwise unused. That is the reason the assertion exists.
    pub board: u32,
```

and, beside `Summary`:

```rust
/// The save-state tag a board's states carry.
///
/// One function with two callers — [`run`]'s assertion and `main`'s `loop_opts` — so
/// that the tag in a [`LoopOpts`] and the board in the [`Machine`] beside it are
/// checkably paired rather than conventionally paired.
///
/// The tags themselves live in `frontend`, next to the codec that writes and checks
/// them. This is only the mapping from a board to its own.
#[must_use]
pub(crate) const fn state_tag(k: machine::BoardKind) -> u32 {
    match k {
        machine::BoardKind::Cps1 => frontend::BOARD_SF2,
        machine::BoardKind::Sf1 => frontend::BOARD_SF1,
    }
}
```

⚠️ **`pub(crate)` and not `pub`.** `main` is in this crate and nothing outside it has a
`LoopOpts` to build. `pub` would put it in `sfemu`'s public surface, and `sfemu` is a
binary — its public surface is the command line.

Then, in `run`, immediately after the parameter list:

```rust
    // `main` picks the board and the tag independently — one from the ROM set, one
    // from `state_tag` — and a `LoopOpts` carrying SF2's tag beside an `Sf1` compiles,
    // runs, and writes states the same machine will then refuse to load. This is the
    // check that they were picked together.
    //
    // `debug_assert` and not `assert`: a mismatch is a bug in `main` that no user
    // input can cause, and a panic here takes someone's window down mid-game.
    debug_assert_eq!(
        o.board,
        state_tag(m.board()),
        "the options' board tag does not match the machine's board"
    );
```

Finally, replace the two `BOARD` uses in `save` and `load` with `o.board` — Step 21 rewrites
both functions wholesale, so this is the same edit seen from two steps.

`run_frame_to_breakpoint`:

```rust
fn run_frame_to_breakpoint(m: &mut Machine, dbg: &mut Debugger) -> bool {
    let start_frames = m.frames();
    // `line` alone cannot say when a frame is done: a breakpoint on the very first
    // instruction of line 0 leaves `line == 0`, which is where the frame started, and a
    // loop watching for the wrap would run a whole extra frame. The frame counter moves
    // exactly once per wrap.
    while m.frames() == start_frames {
        if dbg.should_break(&m.cpu_view()) {
            dbg.note_stopped(&m.cpu_view());
            return true;
        }
        m.step_instruction();
    }
    false
}
```

⚠️ **`m.frames()` and not `m.board.trace.frames`.** `Machine::frames()`'s own doc says it
reads the board's `Trace`, which is the one place either board counts frames — so this and
`m.cpu_view().trace.frames` cannot disagree. Both boards count frames in the same field of
the same shared `Trace` type.

`title_for`:

```rust
fn title_for(s: &Summary, v: &machine::CpuView<'_>, paused: bool, audio: bool) -> String {
```

with `if v.cpu.halted {` in the body, and the call site becoming
`title_for(&summary, &m.cpu_view(), paused, audio.is_running())`.

⚠️ **A `CpuView` and not a `&Machine`.** This function reads exactly one field — `cpu.halted`
— and `CpuView` is the narrow type Task 16 built for precisely that. A `&Machine` parameter
would let a later edit reach the whole board from the title bar.

- [ ] **Step 22: Convert `save`, `load` and `screenshot`**

These are decisions 6, 7 and 8, and they are the reason this task is last.

```rust
/// Writes the machine's state, or notes why it could not.
///
/// # Why the two boards' codecs have different signatures
///
/// CPS-1's [`frontend::encode`] takes the board tag; SF1's [`frontend::encode_sf1`]
/// does not. That is Task 19's decision and it is right: SF1's payload has one
/// hand-counted length, `unframe` validates the tag and the length in the same call,
/// and there is exactly one board that length belongs to. A `board` parameter on
/// `encode_sf1` could only ever be `BOARD_SF1`.
///
/// The consequence is here: `o.board` is read on one arm. [`run`]'s `debug_assert_eq!`
/// is what keeps it honest on the other.
fn save(m: &Machine, o: &LoopOpts, s: &mut Summary) {
    let bytes = match m {
        Machine::Cps1(c) => frontend::encode(&c.snapshot(), o.board),
        Machine::Sf1(f) => frontend::encode_sf1(&f.snapshot()),
    };
    match std::fs::write(&o.state_path, &bytes) {
        Ok(()) => {}
        Err(e) => note(s, format!("cannot write `{}`: {e}", o.state_path.display())),
    }
}
```

`load` keeps its read-then-decode shape, with the decode and the restore both forked:

```rust
fn load(m: &mut Machine, o: &LoopOpts, s: &mut Summary) {
    let bytes = match std::fs::read(&o.state_path) {
        Ok(b) => b,
        Err(e) => {
            note(s, format!("cannot read `{}`: {e}", o.state_path.display()));
            return;
        }
    };
    // The decode and the restore are inside one arm each, not a decode followed by a
    // shared restore: the two codecs return different types — `MachineState` and
    // `Sf1State` — and there is no supertype, deliberately. A state's shape is the
    // board's, and a common one would be a struct with two thirds of its fields unused
    // on whichever board was running.
    match m {
        Machine::Cps1(c) => match frontend::decode(&bytes, o.board) {
            Ok(state) => c.restore(&state),
            Err(e) => note(s, format!("cannot load `{}`: {e}", o.state_path.display())),
        },
        Machine::Sf1(f) => match frontend::decode_sf1(&bytes) {
            Ok(state) => f.restore(&state),
            Err(e) => note(s, format!("cannot load `{}`: {e}", o.state_path.display())),
        },
    }
}
```

⚠️ **The notice text is identical on both arms and duplicated on purpose.** It names the
path and carries the codec's own reason, which is what
`a_corrupt_state_file_does_not_stop_the_loop` asserts on. Factoring it into a closure over
`s` would borrow `s` mutably across both arms while `note` also wants it — and the reader's
question at this line is "what happens when a load fails", which two visible arms answer.

`screenshot`:

```rust
fn screenshot(m: &Machine, o: &LoopOpts, s: &mut Summary) {
    // Two writers, not one: CPS-1's `ppm` asks `Video::rgb()` for a whole RGB buffer
    // and SF1's `ppm_sf1` asks `Sf1Video::rgb(pen)` per pen. Different shapes, because
    // a never-rendered SF1 frame holds CPS-1's `BACKGROUND_PEN` — past SF1's 1,024
    // entries — and the per-pen form answers black for it where an index would be out
    // of bounds. Task 20's `ppm_sf1` doc says so.
    let ppm = match m {
        Machine::Cps1(c) => crate::ppm(&c.video),
        Machine::Sf1(f) => crate::ppm_sf1(&f.video),
    };
    if let Err(e) = std::fs::write(&o.shot_path, &ppm) {
        note(s, format!("cannot write `{}`: {e}", o.shot_path.display()));
    }
}
```

Then, in `crates/sfemu/src/main.rs`, delete the `#[allow(dead_code)]` above `ppm_sf1` and
the `// Wired to `screenshot` in the loop's board fork.` comment beneath it. Task 20's
closing ⚠️ names that attribute as the marker for this step.

- [ ] **Step 25: Write the SF1 mirror of the board-tag test**

Spec item 17 requires "a test that each board's tag refuses the other's state". Task 19
wrote the codec half in `frontend`. This is the loop half, from SF1's side.

```rust
    /// The SF1 loop refuses a CPS-1 state, and keeps running.
    ///
    /// The mirror of `another_boards_state_is_refused_by_the_loop`. Both directions,
    /// because a codec that checked the tag in only one is a codec that lets exactly
    /// one of the two dangerous loads through — and which one depends on which board
    /// the user happens to be playing.
    ///
    /// # Why this fixture is not the CPS-1 one
    ///
    /// It is an [`Sf1`] with a two-byte `jr -2` on each sound CPU and empty graphics
    /// regions: enough to `run_frame` and to snapshot, which is all the loop needs.
    /// See `frontend`'s `an_sf1_machine`, which this mirrors.
    #[test]
    fn a_cps1_state_is_refused_by_an_sf1_loop() {
        let (mut o, _s, _p) = opts("sf1-board-refuse");
        o.board = state_tag(machine::BoardKind::Sf1);
        let mut m = an_sf1_machine();
        m.run_frame();

        // A well-formed CPS-1 state, produced by CPS-1's own codec so that the only
        // thing wrong with it is the board it belongs to. A hand-mangled tag would
        // also be caught by the length check, which is a different check.
        let mut other = machine();
        other.run_frame();
        let bytes = frontend::encode(&cps1(&other).snapshot(), frontend::BOARD_SF2);
        std::fs::write(&o.state_path, &bytes).expect("temp dir is writable");

        let v = m.cpu_view();
        let before = (v.total_cycles, v.cpu.pc, v.line);
        let mut d = Fake::new(vec![(KeySet::from_keys(&[Key::F8]), 0)]);
        let s = run(&mut m, &mut d, &mut NullAudio::default(), &o);
        assert_eq!(s.notices.len(), 1, "{:?}", s.notices);
        let v = m.cpu_view();
        assert_eq!(
            (v.total_cycles, v.cpu.pc, v.line),
            before,
            "the running machine must be untouched by a refused load"
        );
    }
```

⚠️ **`before` is read through `cpu_view()` and dropped before `run`.** A `CpuView` borrows
the machine; holding one across `run(&mut m, …)` is `E0502`. Destructure into a tuple of
`Copy` values, as above, and take a second view afterwards.

⚠️ **The notice's text is not asserted here.** `decode_sf1` refuses this file, but *which*
`StateError` it returns depends on whether the tag or the length is checked first, and
`state.rs`'s documented order puts the board check before the length check on a file long
enough to reach it — CPS-1's payload and SF1's 43,176 bytes are not the same size, so this
file may well be `Truncated` rather than `WrongBoard`. One notice and an untouched machine
is the claim that matters and the one that does not depend on that ordering. The
`WrongBoard`-specifically assertion belongs in `frontend`'s codec tests, where Task 19 put
it, and the CPS-1 sibling above can assert on `"board"` because there both payloads are the
same length.

- [ ] **Step 26: Add the SF1 fixture to the loop's test module**

```rust
    /// An SF1 with just enough program to run a frame.
    ///
    /// Deliberately Task 18's `an_sf1_machine`, byte for byte: a two-instruction
    /// 68000 program, `jr -2` on the FM Z80, a `nop` and `jr -2` on the ADPCM one,
    /// and five empty graphics regions. Two fixtures that differed would leave a
    /// failure here and a pass there with nothing to compare.
    ///
    /// No ROM: the program is written inline and every region is `Vec::new()`.
    fn an_sf1_machine() -> Machine {
        let mut rom = vec![0u8; 0x2000];
        // SSP 0x00FF8000 and PC 0x00001000 — SF1's RAM starts at 0xFF8000, so this is
        // the same shape as the CPS-1 fixture with the board's own RAM top.
        rom[0..8].copy_from_slice(&[0x00, 0xFF, 0xE0, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x10, 0x00]);
        // Vector 0x64 and not 0x68: SF1's vblank is level 1. `sf1/board.rs` says so.
        rom[0x64..0x68].copy_from_slice(&[0x00, 0x00, 0x11, 0x00]);
        rom[0x1000..0x100E].copy_from_slice(&[
            0x46, 0xFC, 0x20, 0x00, 0x52, 0x40, 0x33, 0xC0, 0x00, 0xFF, 0x80, 0x00, 0x60, 0xF6,
        ]);
        rom[0x1100..0x1104].copy_from_slice(&[0x52, 0x41, 0x4E, 0x73]);
        let mut m = machine::Sf1::new(
            &rom,
            machine::video::sf1::Sf1Video::new(
                Vec::new(),
                Vec::new(),
                Vec::new(),
                Vec::new(),
                Vec::new(),
            ),
            vec![0x18, 0xFE],
            vec![0x00, 0x18, 0xFE],
        );
        m.reset();
        Machine::Sf1(Box::new(m))
    }
```

⚠️ **The `move.w` target is 0xFF8000, not CPS-1's 0xFF0000.** The CPS-1 fixture writes to
0x00FF0000, which is SF1's *unmapped* space — `sf1/machine.rs`'s map starts RAM at
0xFF8000. A write there would land in `unmapped_writes` and the counter assertions in
`a_cps1_state_is_refused_by_an_sf1_loop` do not read it, but a fixture that quietly logs an
unmapped write per frame is a trap for the next test written against it. `0xE000` in the
SSP for the same reason: 0x00FFE000 is `objectram`'s base and a stack there is at the top
of RAM, which is where a stack goes.

⚠️ **Task 18's `an_sf1_machine` uses `prog()` where this uses an inline `rom`** — its module
has a `prog()` helper and this one does not. "Byte for byte" above refers to the sound
programs and the empty video regions, which are the parts that matter for a machine that
must merely run. Do not add a `prog()` helper here for one caller.

- [ ] **Step 29: Write the failing test for `--game` and `board_for`**

Spec item 18:

> `main.rs` selects the board rather than hardcoding `games::SF2`, four region lookups,
> `BoardConfig::sf2()` and `Timing::cps1_10mhz()`. **The selection is explicit, not
> inferred from a filename.**

⚠️ **This contradicts a comment Task 18 Step 6 wrote into `main.rs`**, and the spec
governs. That comment reads:

```rust
    // ⚠️ One arm until Task 21 adds the SF1 driver's, at which point the board comes
    // from the ROM set rather than a flag — a user who has sf2.zip should not have to
    // tell the emulator what sf2.zip is.
```

It is wrong, and Step 31 deletes it. The reason the spec is right: a ROM set is a zip of
files whose names — `sf2j.29`, `sf-02.k4` — are the only thing distinguishable about it
without opening every member and matching CRCs, and matching names is precisely
"inferred from a filename" with more steps. `romset::load` already fails loudly and
specifically when the set does not match the spec it was given, naming the file it could
not find, so a wrong `--game` is a clear error rather than a mystery.

**The mechanism: `--game <name>`, defaulting to `sf2`.**

⚠️ **A default is not an inference.** `sf2` is a stated constant in one place, not
something read off the user's path — which is what the spec forbids and what
`default_state_path` is allowed to do (a *state file*'s location may follow the ROM's;
which chip is on the board may not). The default is what keeps every existing invocation,
every existing test and the `SFEMU_ROMS`-gated CPS-1 tests working unchanged, and SF1 asks
for itself: `sfemu /path/to/sf.zip --game sf1 --play`.

⚠️ **The name is validated at parse time, not at load time.** `--game sf3` must be a
usage error naming the games that exist, not a `romset` error about a missing region in a
spec that was never found. That is the difference between "you asked for a game this
program does not know" and "your files are wrong".

Append to `crates/sfemu/src/main.rs`'s test module:

```rust
    /// `--game` selects the board, and the default is stated rather than sniffed.
    #[test]
    fn the_game_option_selects_the_board_and_defaults_to_sf2() {
        let args = |v: Vec<&str>| parse_args(v.into_iter().map(String::from).collect());
        assert_eq!(
            args(vec!["/some/sf2.zip"]).map(|a| a.game).ok(),
            Some("sf2".to_string()),
            "the default is a constant in this program"
        );
        assert_eq!(
            args(vec!["/wherever/it/is.zip", "--game", "sf1"])
                .map(|a| a.game)
                .ok(),
            Some("sf1".to_string()),
            "and a path that says nothing about the board is fine, because the \
             board does not come from the path"
        );
        // Order-independent, like `--ppm` and `--state`.
        assert_eq!(
            args(vec!["--game", "sf1", "/x.zip", "7"]).map(|a| a.game).ok(),
            Some("sf1".to_string())
        );
    }

    /// A game this program does not know is a usage error, not a load error.
    ///
    /// The distinction is the point: `romset::load` on an unknown spec cannot happen
    /// — there is no spec — and a user who typed `sf3` needs to be told which names
    /// exist, not handed a missing-region message about files they got right.
    #[test]
    fn an_unknown_game_name_is_rejected_with_the_names_that_exist() {
        let args = |v: Vec<&str>| parse_args(v.into_iter().map(String::from).collect());
        match args(vec!["/some/set.zip", "--game", "sf3"]) {
            Err(Fault::Failed(m)) => {
                assert!(m.contains("sf3"), "names what was asked for: {m}");
                assert!(m.contains("sf1"), "and both names that exist: {m}");
                assert!(m.contains("sf2"), "{m}");
            }
            other => panic!("expected an error, got {:?}", other.is_ok()),
        }
        // A missing value is an error rather than a silently ignored flag, like the
        // other two options.
        match args(vec!["/some/set.zip", "--game"]) {
            Err(Fault::Failed(m)) => assert_eq!(m, "`--game` needs a name"),
            other => panic!("expected an error, got {:?}", other.is_ok()),
        }
        match args(vec!["/x.zip", "--game", "sf1", "--game", "sf2"]) {
            Err(Fault::Failed(m)) => assert_eq!(m, "`--game` given twice"),
            other => panic!("expected an error, got {:?}", other.is_ok()),
        }
    }

    /// Each name maps to its own board, and to the spec of the same name.
    ///
    /// Two halves, because the failure that matters is a crossed pair: `--game sf1`
    /// loading SF2's ROM spec into SF1's board is a machine that fetches CPS-1 code
    /// through an SF1 bus, and every symptom of that is downstream and confusing.
    #[test]
    fn each_game_name_selects_its_own_board_and_its_own_rom_spec() {
        assert_eq!(board_for("sf2"), Some(machine::BoardKind::Cps1));
        assert_eq!(board_for("sf1"), Some(machine::BoardKind::Sf1));
        assert_eq!(board_for("sf3"), None);
        // And the name that picks the board is the name that picks the files.
        assert_eq!(romset::games::by_name("sf2").map(|g| g.name), Some("sf2"));
        assert_eq!(romset::games::by_name("sf1").map(|g| g.name), Some("sf1"));
    }

    /// The loop's board tag follows the selected game.
    ///
    /// `loop_opts` is where `main`'s two independent choices — the board and the tag
    /// — are made together, and `loop_::run`'s `debug_assert_eq!` is what checks it.
    /// This is the test on this side of that assertion.
    #[test]
    fn the_loops_board_tag_follows_the_selected_game() {
        let a = |game: &str| Args {
            path: "/a/b/set.zip".to_string(),
            frames: 60,
            ppm: None,
            play: true,
            state: PathBuf::from("/tmp/mine.sfs"),
            game: game.to_string(),
        };
        // Literals, not `state_tag(..)`: this asserts what reaches the loop, and
        // calling the function under test to produce the expectation would pass for
        // any mapping at all. Big-endian ASCII `SF2\0` and `SF1\0`.
        assert_eq!(loop_opts(&a("sf2")).board, 0x5346_3200);
        assert_eq!(loop_opts(&a("sf1")).board, 0x5346_3100);
    }
```

```rust
/// The board a game name names.
///
/// Separate from [`romset::games::by_name`] and checked against it by a test: the
/// name selects both the ROM spec and the hardware, and a crossed pair — SF2's files
/// on SF1's bus — is a machine whose every symptom is downstream of a choice made
/// here.
fn board_for(game: &str) -> Option<machine::BoardKind> {
    match game {
        "sf2" => Some(machine::BoardKind::Cps1),
        "sf1" => Some(machine::BoardKind::Sf1),
        _ => None,
    }
}
```
